The first two lemmas below are similar to those in the previous subsection, and enable us to prove the limiting distribution (
42
) of our estimate of
\theta_{0}
in Step
4^{\prime}
, even in the absence of condition
(B)
. By replacing
X_{i}
with
\tilde{W}_{i}
and
\hat{\psi}_{n,j}^{\mathrm{anti}}
with
\check{\psi}_{n,j}
in the proof of Lemma
32
, we obtain the following result.

Lemma 34
.

For
b\equiv(s,a)\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, define

\tilde{R}_{n,j}(b)\equiv\tilde{R}_{n,j}(s,a):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\tilde{W}_{i}\Bigl\{\check{\psi}_{n,j}\Bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\frac{a+\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}s}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)-\hat{\Psi}_{n,j}\Bigl(\frac{a+\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}s}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)\Bigr\}.

If the hypotheses of Theorem
14
are satisfied, then for every
j
and
M>0
, we have

\sup_{b\in B_{M}}\|{\tilde{R}_{n,j}(b)-\tilde{R}_{n,j}(0)}\|=o_{p}(1)

as
n\to\infty
, where
B_{M}=\{b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|{b}\|\leq M\}
.

Lemma 35
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
j\in\{1,2,3\}
and
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl\{\|{\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0}}\|>t\bigr\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\biggl\{\inf_{u\in S^{d-2}}u^{\top}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\tilde{W}_{i}\check{\psi}_{n,j}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\bar{\delta}_{n,j}-t\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}u\bigr)\leq 0\biggr\},

(102)

\displaystyle\bigl\{\|{\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\ddagger}-\theta_{0}}\|>t\bigr\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\biggl\{\inf_{u\in S^{d-2}}u^{\top}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\tilde{W}_{i}\check{\psi}_{n,j}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\bar{\delta}_{n,j}-t\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}u\bigr)\leq 0\biggr\}.

(103)

Consequently, under the hypotheses of Lemma
34
, we have

(a)

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0})=O_{p}(1)
and
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\ddagger}-\theta_{0})=O_{p}(1)
;

(b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\!\!\tilde{W}_{i}\check{\psi}_{n,j}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\bar{\delta}_{n,j}-\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0})\bigr)=o_{p}(1)
and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\!\!\tilde{W}_{i}\check{\psi}_{n,j}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\bar{\delta}_{n,j}-\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\ddagger}-\theta_{0})\bigr)=o_{p}(1)
.

Proof.

Similarly to the proof of Lemma
33
, it suffices to consider
j=1
, and we write
\check{\psi}_{n}\equiv\check{\psi}_{n,1}
and
\hat{\Psi}_{n}\equiv\hat{\Psi}_{n,1}
. By definition,
\hat{\theta}_{n}\equiv\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(1)}
minimises the convex function

\theta\mapsto\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\hat{\ell}_{n,1}\bigl(Y_{i}-\bar{X}_{n,1}^{\top}\bar{\theta}_{n}^{(1)}-\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}\theta\bigr)=\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\check{\ell}_{n}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\bar{\delta}_{n}-\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\theta-\theta_{0})\bigr)=:\check{L}_{n}(\theta)

over
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, where
\check{\ell}_{n}\equiv\hat{\ell}_{n,1}(\cdot+\mu_{0})
and
\bar{\delta}_{n}\equiv\bar{\delta}_{n,1}
. Thus,
h\mapsto\check{L}_{n}\bigl((1-h)\hat{\theta}_{n}+h\theta_{0}\bigr)
is convex and increasing on
[0,\infty)
, so

(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})^{\top}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\tilde{W}_{i}\check{\psi}_{n}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\bar{\delta}_{n}-(1-h)\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})\bigr)\leq 0

for
h\geq 0
. This means that if
\|{\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}}\|>t>0
, then we can take
h=1-t/\|{\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}}\|\in[0,1]
and argue as in (
95
) to obtain (
102
). By similar reasoning based on
\theta\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\check{\ell}_{n}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\bar{\delta}_{n,j}-\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\theta-\theta_{0})\bigr)
, we obtain (
103
).

(a)
By
(A5)
,
\Cov(\tilde{X}_{1})
is positive definite, so its minimum eigenvalue
\lambda_{\min}
is strictly positive. Since
\bar{\theta}_{n}^{(1)}-\theta_{0}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, it follows from
(A5)
that
\bar{\delta}_{n}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})=o_{p}(\gamma_{n}/\alpha_{n})
and hence
V_{n}:=|\bar{\delta}_{n}|+Mn^{-1/2}\max_{i\in I_{3}}\|{\tilde{W}_{i}}\|=o_{p}(\gamma_{n}/\alpha_{n})
as
n\to\infty
. Moreover,
\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\tilde{W}_{i}=0
and
n^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\tilde{W}_{i}\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}\overset{p}{\to}\Cov(\tilde{X}_{1})/3
. Arguing similarly to (
96
), we deduce from (
84
) and Lemma
48
that

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in S^{d-2}}

\displaystyle\biggl\|\,\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\frac{\tilde{W}_{i}}{\sqrt{n}}\hat{\Psi}_{n}\Bigl(\bar{\delta}_{n}+\frac{M\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}u}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)-\frac{Mi^{*}(p_{0})}{3}\Cov(\tilde{X}_{1})u\,\biggr\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{u\in S^{d-2}}\biggl\|\,\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\frac{\tilde{W}_{i}}{\sqrt{n}}\biggl\{\hat{\Psi}_{n}\Bigl(\bar{\delta}_{n}+\frac{M\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}u}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)-\hat{\Psi}_{n}(\bar{\delta}_{n})-\frac{Mi^{*}(p_{0})\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}u}{\sqrt{n}}\biggr\}\,\biggr\|

\displaystyle\hskip 184.9429pt+Mi^{*}(p_{0})\,\biggl\|\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\frac{\tilde{W}_{i}\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}}{n}-\frac{\Cov(\tilde{X}_{1})}{3}\,\biggr\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\|{\tilde{W}_{i}}\|^{2}}{n}\cdot\Delta_{n}(V_{n})+Mi^{*}(p_{0})\,\biggl\|\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\frac{\tilde{W}_{i}\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}}{n}-\frac{\Cov(\tilde{X}_{1})}{3}\,\biggr\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{p}(1).

(104)

Therefore, by Lemma
34
,

\displaystyle\inf_{u\in S^{d-2}}u^{\top}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\frac{\tilde{W}_{i}}{\sqrt{n}}\check{\psi}_{n}\Bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\bar{\delta}_{n}-\frac{M\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}u}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\inf_{u\in S^{d-2}}u^{\top}\biggl\{\tilde{R}_{n,1}(Mu,\sqrt{n}\bar{\delta}_{n})+\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\!\frac{\tilde{W}_{i}}{\sqrt{n}}\hat{\Psi}_{n}\Bigl(\bar{\delta}_{n}+\frac{M\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}u}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{u\in S^{d-2}}u^{\top}\Bigl(\tilde{R}_{n,1}(0,0)+\frac{Mi^{*}(p_{0})}{3}\Cov(\tilde{X}_{1})u\Bigr)+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle\geq-\|{\tilde{R}_{n,1}(0,0)}\|+\frac{M\lambda_{\min}i^{*}(p_{0})}{3}+o_{p}(1).

(105)

Writing
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}\cup\{X_{i}:i\in I_{3}\}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\tilde{W}_{i}\bigl(\check{\psi}_{n}(\varepsilon_{i})-\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})\bigr)\Bigm|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\Bigr)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\tilde{W}_{i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\check{\psi}_{n}-\psi_{0}^{*})\,p_{0}=0,

which together with Lemma
30
implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\Bigl\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\tilde{W}_{i}\bigl(\check{\psi}_{n}(\varepsilon_{i})-\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})\bigr)\Bigr\|^{2}\Bigm|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\|{\tilde{W}_{i}}\|^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl\{(\check{\psi}_{n}-\psi_{0}^{*})^{2}(\varepsilon_{i})\!\bigm|\!\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\|{\tilde{W}_{i}}\|^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\check{\psi}_{n}-\psi_{0}^{*})^{2}\,p_{0}\overset{p}{\to}0.

Moreover, by Lemma
1
,
n^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})\overset{p}{\to}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{0}^{*}\,p_{0}=0
, so writing
\tilde{m}=\mathbb{E}(\tilde{X}_{1})
, we deduce by the central limit theorem that

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{n,1}(0,0)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\tilde{W}_{i}\check{\psi}_{n}(\varepsilon_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\tilde{m})\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})-\sqrt{n}(\bar{X}_{n,1}-\tilde{m})\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\frac{\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})}{n}+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\tilde{m})\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})+o_{p}(1)=O_{p}(1)

(106)

as
n\to\infty
. We have
M\lambda_{\min}i^{*}(p_{0})>0
, so by (
102
) and (
105
),
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\|{\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}}\|\geq M/\sqrt{n})\to 0
as
M\to\infty
, so
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})=O_{p}(1)
as
n\to\infty
. The
\sqrt{n}
-consistency of
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\ddagger}
follows similarly from (
103
).

(b)
Arguing similarly to the proof of Lemma
33
, we deduce from Lemma
46
that

\mathbb{P}\biggl(\max_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\,\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\varepsilon_{i}-\bar{\delta}_{n}-\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\theta-\theta_{0})\in A\}}\leq d-1\biggm|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\biggr)=1,

where
A
denotes the countable set of discontinuities of the decreasing function
\check{\psi}_{n}
. Thus, by
(A5)
and the fact that
\hat{\theta}_{n}\in\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\check{L}_{n}(\theta)
, we have

\Biggl|\,\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\tilde{W}_{i}\check{\psi}_{n}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\bar{\delta}_{n}-\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})\bigr)\Biggr|\leq(d-1)\max_{i\in I_{3}}\|{\tilde{W}_{i}}\|\,\frac{\alpha_{n}}{\gamma_{n}}=o_{p}(n^{1/2})

as
n\to\infty
. The proof of the analogous conclusion for
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\ddagger}
is similar.
∎

To derive the limiting behaviour of the intercept estimate (
38
), we require two additional lemmas whose proofs are slightly more straightforward because unlike the antitonic score estimates
\hat{\psi}_{n,j}
above, the function
\zeta
is not random. This means that sample splitting is not required here. For
i\in[n]
, let
\breve{W}_{i}:=\tilde{X}_{i}-\tilde{m}
and
W_{i}:=(\breve{W}_{i},1)
and note that since
\mathbb{E}(\|{X_{1}}\|^{2})<\infty
, we also have
\mathbb{E}(\|{W_{1}}\|^{2})<\infty
. By a union bound,
n^{-1}\,\mathbb{P}(\max_{i\in[n]}\|{W_{i}}\|^{2}>t)\leq\mathbb{P}(\|{W_{1}}\|^{2}>t)
for all
t>0
. It follows from the dominated convergence theorem that

\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\max_{i\in[n]}\|{W_{i}}\|^{2}\Bigr)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{i\in[n]}\|{W_{i}}\|^{2}>t\Bigr)\,dt\to 0

(107)

as
n\to\infty
; see also
Samworth and Shah, 2025
. Thus,
\max_{i\in[n]}\|{W_{i}}\|=o_{p}(n^{1/2})
.

Lemma 36
.

Suppose that
\zeta\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies
(B)
for some
t_{0}>0
, and that
\mathbb{E}\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})=0
. Define
\Lambda\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\Lambda(t):=\begin{cases}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta(z-t)\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\quad&\text{if }z\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]\\
0\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(108)

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let

R_{n}^{\zeta}(b)\equiv R_{n}^{\zeta}(s,a):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl\{\zeta\Bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\frac{a+\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}s}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)-\Lambda\Bigl(\frac{a+\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}s}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)\Bigr\}.

Then for each
M>0
, we have

\sup_{b\in B_{M}}\|{R_{n}^{\zeta}(b)-R_{n}^{\zeta}(0)}\|=o_{p}(1).

Proof.

This requires only a few minor modifications to the proof of Lemma
34
. Let
\mathcal{W}:=(W_{1},\dotsc,W_{n})
, which is independent of
\varepsilon_{1},\dotsc,\varepsilon_{n}
. Define the event
\Omega_{n}:=\{\max_{i\in[n]}\|{W_{i}}\|\leq t_{0}n^{1/2}/M\}
, which as noted below (
107
) has probability tending to 1 as
n\to\infty
. By
(B)
,
\zeta
satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma
44
, so
\Lambda
is well-defined. Moreover, for each fixed
b\in B_{M}
, we have
\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\zeta(\varepsilon_{i}-W_{i}^{\top}bn^{-1/2})\!\bigm|\!\mathcal{W}\bigr\}=\Lambda(W_{i}^{\top}bn^{-1/2})
for all
i\in[n]
on the event
\Omega_{n}
, so by (
136
) in Lemma
44
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl(\{R_{n}^{\zeta}(b)-R_{n}^{\zeta}(0)\}^{2}\!\bigm|\!\mathcal{W}\bigr)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Var\bigl(\zeta(\varepsilon_{i}-W_{i}^{\top}bn^{-1/2})-\zeta(\varepsilon_{i})\!\bigm|\!\mathcal{W}\bigr)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i\in[n]}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\bigl(\zeta(z-W_{i}^{\top}bn^{-1/2})-\zeta(z)\bigr)^{2}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}\overset{p}{\to}0

(109)

as
n\to\infty
. Thus,
\mathbb{E}\bigl(\{R_{n}^{\zeta}(b)-R_{n}^{\zeta}(0)\}^{2}\!\bigm|\!\mathcal{W}\bigr)=o_{p}(1)
for each
b\in B_{M}
. Fix
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. As in the proof of Lemma
32
, we can find a
\epsilon M
-cover
B_{M,\epsilon}
of
B_{M}
with
|B_{M,\epsilon}|\leq 3\epsilon^{-d}
, and deduce that
\max_{b\in B_{M,\epsilon}}\|{R_{n}^{\zeta}(b)-R_{n}^{\zeta}(0)}\|\overset{p}{\to}0
. For
b^{\prime}\in B_{M,\epsilon}
, Define

r_{n}^{\zeta}(b^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl\{\zeta\biggl(\varepsilon_{i}-\frac{W_{i}^{\top}b^{\prime}+\epsilon M\|{W_{i}}\|}{\sqrt{n}}\biggr)-\zeta\biggl(\varepsilon_{i}-\frac{W_{i}^{\top}b^{\prime}-\epsilon M\|{W_{i}}\|}{\sqrt{n}}\biggr)\biggr\}

Similarly to (
109
), it follows from (
136
) that

\displaystyle\Var\bigl(r_{n}^{\zeta}(b^{\prime})\!\bigm|\!\mathcal{W}\bigr)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{n}}\leq\max_{i\in[n]}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\biggl\{\zeta\biggl(z-\frac{W_{i}^{\top}b^{\prime}+\epsilon M\|{W_{i}}\|}{\sqrt{n}}\biggr)-\zeta\biggl(z-\frac{W_{i}^{\top}b^{\prime}-\epsilon M\|{W_{i}}\|}{\sqrt{n}}\biggr)\biggr\}^{2}p_{0}(z)\,dz\overset{p}{\to}0.

Therefore,
\max_{b^{\prime}\in B_{M,\epsilon}}\bigl\{r_{n}^{\zeta}(b^{\prime})-\mathbb{E}\bigl(r_{n}^{\zeta}(b^{\prime})\!\bigm|\!\mathcal{W}\bigr)\bigr\}=o_{p}(1)
. Furthermore, by
(B)
and (
137
) in Lemma
44
,
\Lambda
is increasing on
[-t_{0},t_{0}]
with
\Lambda^{\prime}(0)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta\in(0,\infty)
and

\Delta_{n}^{\zeta}(v):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in[-v,v]:\\
s\neq t\end{subarray}}\,\biggl|\frac{\Lambda(t)-\Lambda(s)}{t-s}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta\biggr|\to 0

(110)

as
v\to 0
. Consequently, on
\Omega_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{b^{\prime}\in B_{M,\epsilon}}\mathbb{E}\bigl(r_{n}^{\zeta}(b^{\prime})\bigm|\mathcal{W}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\max_{b^{\prime}\in B_{M,\epsilon}}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl\{\Lambda\biggl(\frac{W_{i}^{\top}b^{\prime}+\epsilon M\|{W_{i}}\|}{\sqrt{n}}\biggr)-\Lambda\biggl(\frac{W_{i}^{\top}b^{\prime}-\epsilon M\|{W_{i}}\|}{\sqrt{n}}\biggr)\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{2\epsilon M\|{W_{i}}\|}{n}\Bigl\{\Lambda^{\prime}(0)+\Delta_{n}^{\zeta}\Bigl(\frac{2M\max_{i\in[n]}\|{W_{i}}\|}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=2\epsilon M\Lambda^{\prime}(0)\,\mathbb{E}(\|{W_{1}}\|)+o_{p}(1),

and hence
\max_{b^{\prime}\in B_{M,\epsilon}}\mathbb{E}\bigl(r_{n}^{\zeta}(b^{\prime})\!\bigm|\!\mathcal{W}\bigr)=O_{p}(1)
. On
\Omega_{n}
, we conclude as in the proof of Lemma
32
that

\displaystyle\sup_{b\in B_{M}}\|{R_{n}(b)-R_{n}(0)}\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\max_{b^{\prime}\in B_{M,\epsilon}}\bigl\{r_{n}^{\zeta}(b^{\prime})-\mathbb{E}\bigl(r_{n}^{\zeta}(b^{\prime})\bigm|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\bigr)\bigr\}+2\max_{b^{\prime}\in B_{M,\epsilon}}\mathbb{E}\bigl(r_{n}^{\zeta}(b^{\prime})\bigm|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\bigr)+\max_{b^{\prime}\in B_{M,\epsilon}}\|{R_{n}^{\zeta}(b^{\prime})-R_{n}^{\zeta}(0)}\|

\displaystyle\quad=2\epsilon M\Lambda^{\prime}(0)\,\mathbb{E}(\|{W_{1}}\|)+o_{p}(1).

Since
\epsilon\in(0,1)
was arbitrary and
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{n})\to 1
, the result follows.
∎

Lemma 37
.

Suppose that
\zeta\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies
(B)
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\hat{\mu}_{n}^{\zeta}
be the intercept estimate in (
38
) based on
\hat{\theta}_{n}\in\{\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\dagger},\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\ddagger}\}
and define
\hat{\delta}_{n}:=\hat{\mu}_{n}^{\zeta}-\mu_{0}+\tilde{m}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})
. Then for
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl\{|\hat{\delta}_{n}|>t\bigr\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\biggl\{\min_{u\in\{-1,1\}}u\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-tu\bigr)\leq 0\biggr\}.

(111)

Moreover, if
\mathbb{E}\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})=0
, then
\sqrt{n}\hat{\delta}_{n}=O_{p}(1)
and
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)=o_{p}(1)
.

Proof.

By (
38
),
\hat{\mu}_{n}^{\zeta}=\hat{\delta}_{n}+\mu_{0}-\tilde{m}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})
minimises

\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{\zeta}(Y_{i}-\tilde{X}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{n}-\mu)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{\zeta}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-(\breve{W}_{i}+\tilde{m})^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-(\mu-\mu_{0})\bigr)

over
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, so
\hat{\delta}_{n}
minimises the convex function

\alpha\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{\zeta}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-\alpha\bigr)=:G_{n}(\alpha)

(112)

over
\mathbb{R}
. For
t>0
, if
\hat{\delta}_{n}>t
, then
0\geq G_{n}^{(\mathrm{R})}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-t\bigr)
, whereas if
\hat{\delta}_{n}<-t
, then
0\leq G_{n}^{(\mathrm{R})}(-t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})+t\bigr)
. This yields (
111
).

Next, fix
M>0
. By Lemma
35
(a)
,
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})=O_{p}(1)
, which together with (
107
) implies that
V_{n}:=Mn^{-1/2}+\max_{i\in[n]}\|{W_{i}}\|\,|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}|=o_{p}(1)
. Since
\Lambda(0)=\mathbb{E}\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})=0
and
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\breve{W}_{i}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\tilde{m})=O_{p}(1)
, we deduce from (
110
) and Lemma
48
that

\displaystyle\max_{u\in\{-1,1\}}\,

\displaystyle\biggl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Lambda\Bigl(\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})+\frac{Mu}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)+Mu\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{u\in\{-1,1\}}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,\biggl|\Lambda\Bigl(\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})+\frac{Mu}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)-\Lambda^{\prime}(0)\Bigl(\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})+\frac{Mu}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)\biggr|+O_{p}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sqrt{n}\|{W_{i}}\|\,|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}|+M}{n}\cdot\Delta_{n}^{\zeta}(V_{n})+O_{p}(n^{-1/2})=o_{p}(1).

(113)

By Lemma
36
and (
113
),

\displaystyle\min_{u\in\{-1,1\}}

\displaystyle u\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta\Bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-\frac{Mu}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\min_{u\in\{-1,1\}}u\,\biggl\{R_{n}^{\zeta}\bigl(\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}),Mu\bigr)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda\Bigl(\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})+\frac{Mu}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\min_{u\in\{-1,1\}}u\Bigl(R_{n}^{\zeta}(0,0)-Mu\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta\Bigr)+o_{p}(1)=-M\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta-|R_{n}^{\zeta}(0,0)|+o_{p}(1).

Now
R_{n}^{\zeta}(0,0)=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta(\varepsilon_{i})=O_{p}(1)
and
-\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta\in(0,\infty)
, so by (
111
),
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\hat{\delta}_{n}>M/\sqrt{n})\to 0
as
M\to\infty
, i.e.
\sqrt{n}\hat{\delta}_{n}=O_{p}(1)
.

For the final assertion, we have
\max_{i\in[n]}|\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})+\hat{\delta}_{n}|=o_{p}(1)
, so letting
\xi(z):=\lim_{z^{\prime}\nearrow z}\zeta(z^{\prime})-\zeta(z)=\max\{|a-b|:a,b\in\partial L_{\zeta}(z)\}
for
z\in\mathbb{R}
, we deduce that

\max_{i\in[n]}\,\xi\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)\leq\max_{i\in[n]}\,\bigl(|\zeta(\varepsilon_{i}-t_{0})|+|\zeta(\varepsilon_{i}+t_{0})|\bigr)

with probability tending to 1 as
n\to\infty
. By
(B)
and (
107
), it follows that

\max_{i\in[n]}\,\xi\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)=o_{p}(n^{1/2}).

(114)

Moreover, the set
A:=\{z\in\mathbb{R}:\xi(z)>0\}
of discontinuities of
\zeta
is countable, and
(\varepsilon_{1},\dotsc,\varepsilon_{n})
has a density with respect to Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, so as in (
99
), we deduce from Lemma
46
that

\mathbb{P}\biggl(\max_{\mu\in\mathbb{R},\,\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\varepsilon_{i}-\mu-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}\theta\in A\}}\leq d\biggm|\mathcal{W}\biggr)=1

almost surely. Since
\hat{\delta}_{n}
minimises the convex function
\alpha\mapsto G_{n}(\alpha)
in (
112
) over
\mathbb{R}
, we must have
0\in\partial G_{n}(\hat{\delta}_{n})=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial L_{\zeta}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)
. Therefore,

\Biggl|\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)\Biggr|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)\leq d\max_{i\in[n]}\,\xi\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)

almost surely, so the conclusion follows by (
114
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
14
.

By Lemma
6
,
\lim_{z\to-\infty}\psi_{0}^{*}(z)>0>\lim_{z\to\infty}\psi_{0}^{*}(z)
, and for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, we have
\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\check{\psi}_{n,j}-\psi_{0}^{*})^{2}\,p_{0}\overset{p}{\to}0
by (
82
). Thus, with probability tending to 1 as
n\to\infty
, we have
\lim_{z\to-\infty}\check{\psi}_{n,j}(z)>0>\lim_{z\to\infty}\check{\psi}_{n,j}(z)
. In this case, there exists
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}
satisfying (
37
) for each
j
, and by Lemma
35
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\tilde{W}_{i}\hat{\Psi}_{n,j}\bigl(\bar{\delta}_{n,j}

\displaystyle+\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0})\bigr)+\tilde{R}_{n,j}\bigl(\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0}),\sqrt{n}\bar{\delta}_{n,j}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\tilde{W}_{i}\check{\psi}_{n,j}\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\bar{\delta}_{n,j}-\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0})\bigr)=o_{p}(1)

(115)

and

\biggl\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\tilde{W}_{i}\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0})\biggr\|\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\|{\tilde{W}_{i}}\|^{2}\|{\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0})}\|=O_{p}(1).

Moreover, by
(A5)
and Lemma
35
(a)
,
\bigl(|\bar{\delta}_{n,j}|+\max_{i\in I_{j+2}}|\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0})|\bigr)\,\alpha_{n}/\gamma_{n}=o_{p}(1)
as
n\to\infty
. Hence, using the fact that
\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\tilde{W}_{i}=0
and arguing similarly to (
104
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\tilde{W}_{i}\hat{\Psi}_{n,j}\bigl(\bar{\delta}_{n,j}+\tilde{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{i^{*}(p_{0})}{3}\Sigma\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0})+o_{p}(1),

(116)

where
\Sigma=\Cov(\tilde{X}_{1})
. Therefore, by (
116
) and (
115
) followed by Lemmas
34
and
35
(a)
, and then (
106
),

\displaystyle\frac{i^{*}(p_{0})}{3}\Sigma\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0})

\displaystyle=-\tilde{R}_{n,j}\bigl(\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0}),\sqrt{n}\bar{\delta}_{n,j}\bigr)+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=-\tilde{R}_{n,j}(0,0)+o_{p}(1)=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\tilde{m})\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})+o_{p}(1)

for each
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, where
\tilde{m}=\mathbb{E}(\tilde{X}_{1})
. By the central limit theorem, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\dagger}-\theta_{0})=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{\sqrt{n}}{3}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}-\theta_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Sigma^{-1}}{i^{*}(p_{0})\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\tilde{m})\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Sigma^{-1}}{i^{*}(p_{0})\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\tilde{m})\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})+o_{p}(1)\overset{d}{\to}N_{d-1}\biggl(0,\,\frac{\Sigma^{-1}}{i^{*}(p_{0})}\biggr)

(117)

as
n\to\infty
. By similar reasoning based on Lemmas
34
and
35
,
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\ddagger}-\theta_{0})
has the same limiting distribution.

Next, taking
\hat{\theta}_{n}\in\{\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\dagger},\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\ddagger}\}
, we deduce from Lemma
37
that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda\bigl(\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})+\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)+R_{n}^{\zeta}\bigl(\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}),\sqrt{n}\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})-\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)=o_{p}(1)

and moreover that
|\hat{\delta}_{n}|+\max_{i\in[n]}|\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})|=o_{p}(1)
as
n\to\infty
. Therefore, arguing similarly to (
113
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda\bigl(\breve{W}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})+\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)=-\sqrt{n}\hat{\delta}_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta+o_{p}(1).

By applying Lemmas
36
and
37
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\hat{\delta}_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta

\displaystyle=R_{n}^{\zeta}\bigl(\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}),\sqrt{n}\hat{\delta}_{n}\bigr)+o_{p}(1)=R_{n}^{\zeta}(0,0)+o_{p}(1)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta(\varepsilon_{i})+o_{p}(1).

(118)

Since
\tilde{X_{1}}
has mean
\tilde{m}
and is independent of
\varepsilon_{1}
, we have

\Cov\bigl(\zeta(\varepsilon_{1}),(\tilde{X}_{1}-\tilde{m})\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=\mathbb{E}(\tilde{X}_{1}-\tilde{m})\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=0.

Thus, since
\upsilon_{p_{0}}=(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta^{2}\,p_{0})/(\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta)^{2}\in(0,\infty)
by
(B)
, we conclude from (
117
) and (
118
) that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\beta}_{n}-\beta_{0})=\sqrt{n}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}\\
\hat{\delta}_{n}-\tilde{m}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\Sigma^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\tilde{m})}{i^{*}(p_{0})}\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})\\[8.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl(\frac{\zeta(\varepsilon_{i})}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta}+\frac{\tilde{m}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\tilde{m})}{i^{*}(p_{0})}\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})\biggr)\end{pmatrix}+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle\overset{d}{\to}N_{d}\bigl(0,(\tilde{I}_{\beta_{0}}^{*})^{-1}\bigr),

where
(\tilde{I}_{\beta_{0}}^{*})^{-1}
is the limiting covariance matrix in (
41
).
∎

6.3.5
Semiparametric calculations for Section
3.4

The purpose of this subsection is to formalise the semiparametric model underlying Theorem
14
, and derive the efficient score functions and information matrices that underpin our theory and methodology. Suppose that
X_{1}=(\tilde{X}_{1},1)
has a density
q_{X}
with respect to some
\sigma
-finite measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then for
\beta_{0}=(\theta_{0},\mu_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}
, the independence of
X_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{1}
means that the joint density of
(X_{1},Y_{1})
in the linear model (
36
) with respect to
\nu\otimes\mathrm{Leb}
is

(x,y)\mapsto q_{\beta_{0},p_{0}}(x,y)=q_{X}(x)\,p_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0}).

Denote by
\mathcal{Q}
the set of all such joint densities
q_{\beta_{0},p_{0}}
under which
\mathbb{E}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\top})
is invertible and
i(p_{0})<\infty
. For a fixed decreasing, right-continuous function
\zeta\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\inf_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\zeta(z)<0<\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\zeta(z)
, define
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}
to be the subclass of
\mathcal{Q}
for which the density
p_{0}
satisfies condition
(B)
with
\varphi=\zeta
; this is the model in Section
3.4
.
Both
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}
are
separated semiparametric models

(
van der Vaart, 1998
, Section 25.4)
in which
p_{0}
is regarded as an unknown nuisance parameter. In the former, the intercept
\mu_{0}
is unidentifiable and hence also treated as a nuisance parameter.

Proposition 38
.

Let
m:=\mathbb{E}(\tilde{X}_{1})
and
\Sigma:=\Cov(\tilde{X}_{1})
.

(a)

In the model
\mathcal{Q}
, the efficient score function for
\theta_{0}
is

(x,y)\mapsto\tilde{\ell}_{\theta_{0}}(x,y):=-(\tilde{x}-\tilde{m})\,\psi_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})

(119)

and the corresponding efficient information matrix is
\tilde{I}_{\theta_{0}}=i(p_{0})\Sigma
.

(b)

In the model
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}
, the efficient score function for
\beta_{0}
is

\tilde{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}(x,y)=-\bigl(x-\mathbb{E}(X_{1})\bigr)\psi_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})+\frac{\mathbb{E}(X_{1})\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta^{2}p_{0}}\,\zeta(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0}),

(120)

and the corresponding efficient information matrix and its inverse are

\tilde{I}_{\beta_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}i(p_{0})\Sigma+\tilde{m}\tilde{m}^{\top}/\upsilon_{p_{0}}&\tilde{m}/\upsilon_{p_{0}}\\[3.0pt]
\tilde{m}^{\top}/\upsilon_{p_{0}}&1/\upsilon_{p_{0}}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\quad\tilde{I}_{\beta_{0}}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{\Sigma^{-1}}{i(p_{0})}&-\dfrac{\Sigma^{-1}\tilde{m}}{i(p_{0})}\\[10.0pt]
-\dfrac{\tilde{m}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}}{i(p_{0})}&\upsilon_{p_{0}}+\dfrac{\tilde{m}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\tilde{m}}{i(p_{0})}\end{pmatrix}.

(121)

For completeness, we will prove this result in full. The model
\mathcal{Q}
is the focus of
Bickel, 1982
, while
Kosorok, 2008
considers two special cases of
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}
where the errors either have mean 0 or median 0, corresponding to the functions
\zeta\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
given by
\zeta(z)=-z
and
\zeta(z)=-\sgn(z)
respectively. The calculations for general
\zeta
are similar.

Proof.

Fix
q_{\beta_{0},p_{0}}\in\mathcal{Q}
for some
\beta_{0},p_{0}
as above, and write
Q_{0}
for the corresponding distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
. To define a path in
\mathcal{Q}
, take
p_{1}
to be any other Lebesgue density on
\mathbb{R}
such that
i(p_{1})<\infty
, and let
\gamma:=p_{1}-p_{0}
. For
b=(s,a)\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}
and
t\in[0,1]
, the function
p_{t}:=p_{0}+t\gamma=(1-t)p_{0}+tp
is a density, so

(x,y)\mapsto q_{\beta_{0}+tb,p_{t}}(x,y)=q_{X}(x)\,p_{t}\bigl(y-x^{\top}(\beta_{0}+tb)\bigr)

is a joint density that belongs to
\mathcal{Q}
. Then
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log q_{\beta_{0}+tb,p_{t}}(x,y)\Big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=-b^{\top}x\psi_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})+\frac{\gamma}{p_{0}}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})

\displaystyle=-s^{\top}\tilde{x}\psi_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})-a\psi_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})+\frac{\gamma}{p_{0}}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0}),

(122)

where here and below,
\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
denotes the subvector comprising the first
d-1
components of
x
. The first two terms on the right-hand side of (
122
) indicate that in a parametric submodel where
p_{0}
is known, the score function for
\beta_{0}
is

(x,y)\mapsto-x\psi_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})=:\dot{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}\dot{\ell}_{\theta_{0}}(x,y)\\
\dot{\ell}_{\mu_{0}}(x,y)\end{pmatrix},

which decomposes into the parametric scores for
\theta_{0}
and
\mu_{0}
given by

\dot{\ell}_{\theta_{0}}(x,y):=-\tilde{x}\psi_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{\ell}_{\mu_{0}}(x,y):=-\psi_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})

respectively. If
(X_{1},Y_{1})\sim Q_{0}
, then
\mathbb{E}\bigl(\|{\dot{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}(X_{1},Y_{1})}\|^{2}\bigr)=\mathbb{E}(\|{X_{1}}\|^{2})\,i(p_{0})<\infty
, so
\dot{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}\in L^{2}(Q_{0})^{d}
. The third term in (
122
) yields an element
(x,y)\mapsto\Gamma(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})
of the
nuisance tangent set

\mathcal{N}
for
p_{0}

(
van der Vaart, 1998
, Section 25.4)
,
where
\Gamma:=\gamma/p_{0}
.

(a)
In the model
\mathcal{Q}
, the parameter of interest is
\theta_{0}
while both
\mu_{0}
and
p_{0}
are nuisance parameters. We have
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma(z)\,p_{0}(z)\,dz=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma=0
above, so considering the second and third terms in (
122
) and arguing as in
van der Vaart, 1998
, we deduce that the full nuisance tangent set is

\mathcal{N}=\bigl\{(x,y)\mapsto a\psi_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})+\Gamma(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0}):a\in\mathbb{R},\,\Gamma\in L^{2}(P_{0})\bigr\}.

By definition, the efficient score function for
\theta_{0}
is the
L^{2}(Q_{0})
orthogonal projection of
\dot{\ell}_{\theta_{0}}
onto the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{N}
,
which comprises functions of
y-x^{\top}\beta_{0}
only. By the independence of
X_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl\{(\tilde{X}_{1}-\tilde{m})\psi_{0}(\varepsilon_{1})\cdot g(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr\}=0

(123)

for all
g\in L^{2}(P_{0})
. Moreover, every component of
(x,y)\mapsto\tilde{m}\psi_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})=-\tilde{m}\dot{\ell}_{\mu_{0}}(x,y)
belongs to
\mathcal{N}
, so

(x,y)\mapsto\tilde{\ell}_{\theta_{0}}(x,y):=-(\tilde{x}-\tilde{m})\psi_{0}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})

is indeed the efficient score function for
\theta_{0}
in the model
\mathcal{Q}
. Consequently, the efficient information matrix for
\theta_{0}
is

\tilde{I}_{\theta_{0}}:=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\tilde{\ell}_{\theta_{0}}(X_{1},Y_{1})\,\tilde{\ell}_{\theta_{0}}(X_{1},Y_{1})^{\top}\bigr)=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi_{0}(\varepsilon_{1})^{2}\bigr)\Cov(\tilde{X}_{1})=i(p_{0})\Sigma.

(b)
In the model
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}
, the parameter of interest is
\beta_{0}
and the nuisance parameter is
p_{0}
. Returning to (
122
), we begin with
q_{\beta_{0},p_{0}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}
and must ensure that
q_{\beta_{0}+tb,p_{t}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. This restricts us to consider Lebesgue densities
p_{1}
with
i(p_{1})<\infty
that also satisfy
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta(z)\,p_{1}(z)\,dz=0
and
\upsilon_{p_{1}}\in(0,\infty)
. In this case,
\gamma=p_{1}-p_{0}
satisfies
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(z)\,dz=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta(z)\gamma(z)\,dz=0
, and arguments similar to those for
(a)
show that the full nuisance tangent set is

\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}=\bigl\{(x,y)\mapsto\Gamma(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0}):\Gamma\in L^{2}(P_{0}),\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(\Gamma(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})\Gamma(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=0\bigr\},

which is a closed subspace of
L^{2}(Q_{0})
. Indeed,
\mathcal{U}:=\{c+d\zeta:c,d\in\mathbb{R}\}
is a finite-dimensional (and hence closed) subspace of
L^{2}(P_{0})
whose orthogonal complement
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}
is also closed, and we can write
\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}=\bigl\{(x,y)\mapsto\Gamma(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0}):\Gamma\in\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\}
.

Consider a square-integrable random variable
\phi(X_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
such that
\mathbb{E}\bigl(\phi(X_{1},\varepsilon_{1})\,\Gamma(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=0
for all
\Gamma\in L^{2}(P_{0})
satisfying
\mathbb{E}\bigl(\Gamma(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})\Gamma(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=0
. Then by the tower property of expectation,
\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathbb{E}\{\phi(X_{1},\varepsilon_{1})\,|\,\varepsilon_{1}\}\cdot\Gamma(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=0
for all
\Gamma\in\mathcal{U}^{\perp}
, so because
(\mathcal{U}^{\perp})^{\perp}=\mathcal{U}
, there exist
c,d\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\mathbb{E}\{\phi(X_{1},\varepsilon_{1})\,|\,\varepsilon_{1}\}=c+d\zeta(\varepsilon_{1}).

Moreover,

\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi_{0}(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}^{\prime}(z)\,dz=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\bigl(\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})\psi_{0}(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta(z)p_{0}^{\prime}(z)\,dz=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta,

where the final equality follows from Fubini’s theorem, similarly to (
135
). Write
(x,y)\mapsto\Gamma^{*}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})
for the componentwise
L^{2}(Q_{0})
orthogonal projection of
\dot{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}
onto
\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}
. Then each of the
d
components of
\dot{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}(X_{1},Y_{1})-\Gamma^{*}(\varepsilon_{1})
is a function of the form
\phi(X_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
with the above properties, so

\Gamma^{*}(\varepsilon_{1})=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\dot{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}(X_{1},Y_{1})\,|\,\varepsilon_{1}\bigr)-c^{*}-d^{*}\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})=-\mathbb{E}(X_{1})\psi_{0}(\varepsilon_{1})-c^{*}-d^{*}\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})

for
c^{*},d^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying

\displaystyle 0=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\Gamma^{*}(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=-c^{*},\qquad 0=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})\Gamma^{*}(\varepsilon_{1})\bigr)=\mathbb{E}(X_{1})\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta-d^{*}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta^{2}p_{0}.

By definition, the efficient score function for
\beta_{0}
in
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}
is the componentwise
L^{2}(Q_{0})
orthogonal projection
\tilde{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}
of
\dot{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}
onto the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}
, so
\tilde{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}(x,y)=\dot{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}(x,y)-\Gamma^{*}(y-x^{\top}\beta_{0})
is indeed given by (
120
). The efficient information matrix for
\beta_{0}
is

\displaystyle\tilde{I}_{\beta_{0}}:=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\tilde{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}(X_{1},Y_{1})\,\tilde{\ell}_{\beta_{0}}(X_{1},Y_{1})^{\top}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(X_{1}-\mathbb{E}(X_{1})\bigr)\bigl(X_{1}-\mathbb{E}(X_{1})\bigr)^{\top}\bigr\}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi_{0}(\varepsilon_{1})^{2}\bigr)+\frac{\mathbb{E}(X_{1})\mathbb{E}(X_{1})^{\top}}{\upsilon_{p_{0}}},

which simplifies to the expression in (
121
). Therefore, the block matrix inversion formula
(e.g.
Samworth and Shah, 2025
, Proposition 10.10.2)
yields the expression for
\tilde{I}_{\beta_{0}}^{-1}
.
∎

By Proposition
38
(b)
, the efficient score equations for estimating
\beta_{0}
in the model
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}
based on
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\dotsc,(X_{n},Y_{n})
and a plug-in estimate
\tilde{\psi}_{n}
of
\psi_{0}
are given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}-\bar{X}_{n})\,\tilde{\psi}_{n}(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{\top}\beta)-\frac{\bar{X}_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta^{2}p_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{\top}\beta)=0

for
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\bar{X}_{n}:=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
. Since
X_{id}=1
for all
i\in[n]
, this system is equivalent to

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\bar{\tilde{X}}_{n})\,\tilde{\psi}_{n}(Y_{i}-\mu-\tilde{X}_{i}^{\top}\theta)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta(Y_{i}-\mu-\tilde{X}_{i}^{\top}\theta)=0,

(124)

where
\bar{\tilde{X}}_{n}:=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{X}_{i}
. Our estimator
\hat{\beta}_{n}=(\hat{\theta}_{n},\hat{\mu}_{n}^{\zeta})
of
\beta=(\theta_{0},\mu_{0})
in Theorem
14
solves a variant of (
124
) where
\tilde{\psi}_{n}
is a kernel-based estimate of the antitonic projected score
\psi_{0}^{*}
instead of
\psi_{0}
.

6.3.6
Comparison with composite quantile regression

Zou and Yuan, 2008
introduced a robust alternative to least squares called the
composite quantile regression estimator
, which remains
\sqrt{n}
-consistent when the error variance is infinite, but also has asymptotic efficiency at least 70% that of OLS under suitable conditions on the error density
p_{0}
. This is achieved by borrowing strength across several quantiles of the conditional distribution of
Y_{1}
given
X_{1}
, rather than targeting just a single quantile (e.g. the conditional median) using the
quantile loss

\ell_{\tau}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
given by
\ell_{\tau}(z):=(\tau-\mathbbm{1}_{\{z<0\}})z
for
\tau\in(0,1)
. More precisely, for
K\in(0,\infty)
, let
\tau_{k}:=k/(K+1)
for
k\in[K]
and define

(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\mathrm{CQ},K},\hat{\mu}_{n,1},\dotsc,\hat{\mu}_{n,K})\in\argmin_{(\theta,\mu_{1},\dotsc,\mu_{K})}\,\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{\tau_{k}}(Y_{i}-\mu_{k}-\tilde{X}_{i}^{\top}\theta),

(125)

where the
\argmin
is taken over all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
and
\mu_{1},\dotsc,\mu_{K}\in\mathbb{R}
. Under assumption (2) of
Zou and Yuan, 2008
in our random design setting, it follows from their Theorems 2.1 and 3.1 that

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\mathrm{CQ},K}-\theta_{0})\overset{d}{\to}N_{d-1}\bigl(0,\,V_{p_{0},\mathrm{CQ},K}\cdot\Cov(\tilde{X}_{1})^{-1}\bigr),

where writing
J_{0}=p_{0}\circ F_{0}^{-1}
for the density quantile function of the errors, we have

V_{p_{0},\mathrm{CQ},K}:=\frac{\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=1}^{K}\tau_{k\wedge k^{\prime}}(1-\tau_{k\vee k^{\prime}})}{\bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{K}J_{0}(\tau_{k})\bigr)^{2}}\to\frac{1}{12\,\bigl(\int_{0}^{1}J_{0}\bigr)^{2}}=:V_{p_{0},\mathrm{CQ}}

as
K\to\infty
. For every
p_{0}
satisfying their assumptions,
Zou and Yuan, 2008
established that the CQR estimator (in the notional limit
K\to\infty
) has asymptotic relative efficiency

\frac{V_{p_{0}}(\psi_{\mathrm{OLS}})}{V_{p_{0},\mathrm{CQ}}}=12\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}J_{0}\biggr)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}z^{2}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz>\frac{6}{e\pi}\approx 0.703

relative to OLS, where
\psi_{\mathrm{OLS}}(z):=-z
for
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Nevertheless, Theorem
14
and the following result imply that the asymptotic covariance of the ‘limiting’ CQR estimator is always at least that of the semiparametric convex
M
-estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}
in our framework. Moreover, the former estimator can have arbitrarily low efficiency relative to the latter, even when
p_{0}
is log-concave.

Lemma 39
.

For every uniformly continuous density
p_{0}
, we have
V_{p_{0},\mathrm{CQ}}\geq 1/i^{*}(p_{0})
, with equality if and only if either
i^{*}(p_{0})=\infty
or
p_{0}
is a logistic density of the form

p_{0}(z)=\frac{\lambda e^{-\lambda(z-\mu)}}{(1+e^{-\lambda(z-\mu)})^{2}}

for
z\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda>0
.
Moreover,

\inf_{p_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{LC}}:i(p_{0})<\infty}\,\frac{1}{i^{*}(p_{0})\,V_{p_{0},\mathrm{CQ}}}=0.

Proof.

By
Rockafellar, 1997
and (
44
), we have
\hat{J}_{0}(v)=\int_{0}^{v}\hat{J}_{0}^{(\mathrm{R})}
for all
v\in[0,1]
, so applying Fubini’s theorem and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality yields

\displaystyle\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}J_{0}\biggr)^{2}\leq\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}\hat{J}_{0}\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle=\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\hat{J}_{0}^{(\mathrm{R})}(u)\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\leq v\}}\,du\,dv\biggr)^{2}=\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}(1-u)\,\hat{J}_{0}^{(\mathrm{R})}(u)\,du\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle=\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}-u\Bigr)\,\hat{J}_{0}^{(\mathrm{R})}(u)\,du\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}-u\Bigr)^{2}\,du\biggr)\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(\hat{J}_{0}^{(\mathrm{R})}\bigr)^{2}=\frac{1}{12}\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(\hat{J}_{0}^{(\mathrm{R})}\bigr)^{2},

where the equality in the second line holds because
\int_{0}^{1}\hat{J}_{0}^{(\mathrm{R})}=\hat{J}_{0}(1)=0
. Thus, by Remark
3
,

i^{*}(p_{0})=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(\hat{J}_{0}^{(\mathrm{R})}\bigr)^{2}\geq 12\,\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}J_{0}\biggr)^{2}=\frac{1}{V_{p_{0},\mathrm{CQ}}},

and equality holds if and only if
J_{0}=\hat{J}_{0}
and there exists
\lambda>0
such that
\hat{J}_{0}^{(\mathrm{R})}(u)=\lambda(1-2u)
for
u\in[0,1]
, i.e.
J_{0}(u)=\lambda u(1-u)
for all such
u
. Now by direct calculation, the logistic density
q_{\lambda}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

q_{\lambda}(z):=\frac{\lambda e^{-\lambda z}}{(1+e^{-\lambda z})^{2}}

has corresponding quantile function
J_{0}
. Since
J_{0}>0
on
(0,1)
, it follows from the last assertion of Lemma
17
that
p_{0}(\cdot)=q_{\lambda}(\cdot-\mu)
for some
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, as claimed.

Finally, given
\epsilon\in(0,1/2]
, define the log-concave density
p_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

p_{0}(z):=\exp\biggl(\frac{(1-2\epsilon-2|z|)\wedge 0}{2\epsilon}\biggr).

Then the corresponding density quantile function
J_{0}=p_{0}\circ F_{0}^{-1}
satisfies

J_{0}(u)=\begin{cases}\min(u/\epsilon,1)\;&\text{for }u\in[0,1/2]\\
J_{0}(1-u)\;&\text{for }u\in[1/2,1],\end{cases}

so
i(p_{0})=i^{*}(p_{0})=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(\hat{J}_{0}^{(\mathrm{R})}\bigr)^{2}=2/\epsilon<\infty
and hence

\frac{1}{i^{*}(p_{0})\,V_{p_{0},\mathrm{CQ}}}=\frac{12\,\bigl(\int_{0}^{1}J_{0}\bigr)^{2}}{\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(\hat{J}_{0}^{(\mathrm{R})}\bigr)^{2}}=6\epsilon(1-\epsilon)^{2}.

By taking
\epsilon
to be arbitrarily small, we obtain the final assertion of the lemma.
∎

6.3.7
Proofs for Section
3.5

Proof of Lemma
15
.

First consider the setting of Theorem
13
and let
j=1
. Defining the residuals
\check{\varepsilon}_{i}:=\varepsilon_{i}+X_{i}^{\top}b_{n}
for
i\in I_{3}
, we have that
(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})_{i\in I_{3}}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\prime}=\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}\cup(X_{i})_{i\in I_{3}}
, where
b_{n}:=\beta_{0}-\bar{\beta}_{n}^{(1)}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
by assumption. Thus, by
(A5)
and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,
\max_{i\in I_{3}}|X_{i}^{\top}b_{n}|\,\alpha_{n}/\gamma_{n}=o_{p}(1)
. Let
\hat{\psi}_{n}\in\{\hat{\psi}_{n,1},\hat{\psi}_{n,1}^{\mathrm{anti}}\}
. Then

\displaystyle\biggl|\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\hat{\psi}_{n}(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle-\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggr|\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\bigl(\hat{\psi}_{n}(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})-\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\bigl(\hat{\psi}_{n}(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})-\hat{\psi}_{n}(\varepsilon_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}+\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\bigl(\hat{\psi}_{n}(\varepsilon_{i})-\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}.

(126)

Similarly to (
92
), it follows from (
83
) in Lemma
30
together with Corollary
31
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\bigl(\hat{\psi}_{n}(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})-\hat{\psi}_{n}(\varepsilon_{i})\bigr)^{2}\Bigm|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\Bigr)\leq\max_{i\in I_{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\hat{\psi}_{n}(z+X_{i}^{\top}b_{n})-\hat{\psi}_{n}(z)\bigr)^{2}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\overset{p}{\to}0

(127)

as
n\to\infty
. Moreover, by (
82
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\bigl(\hat{\psi}_{n}(\varepsilon_{i})-\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})\bigr)^{2}\Bigm|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\Bigr)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\hat{\psi}_{n}-\psi_{0}^{*})^{2}\,p_{0}\overset{p}{\to}0,

(128)

so together with Lemma
47
and the weak law of large numbers, it follows from (
126
)–(
128
) that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\hat{\psi}_{n}(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})^{2}+o_{p}(1)=\frac{i^{*}(p_{0})}{3}+o_{p}(1).

(129)

Arguing similarly for
j\in\{2,3\}
, we conclude that
\hat{\imath}_{n}\overset{p}{\to}i^{*}(p_{0})
under the hypotheses of Theorem
13
.

The proof is similar in the setting of Theorem
14
, except that when
j=1
we instead take
\hat{\psi}_{n}(\cdot)=\hat{\psi}_{n,1}(\cdot+\mu_{0})
and use residuals
\check{\varepsilon}_{i}:=Y_{i}-\tilde{X}_{i}^{\top}\bar{\theta}_{n}^{(1)}=\varepsilon_{i}+\mu_{0}+X_{i}^{\top}b_{n}
that are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
for
i\in[n]
, where
b_{n}:=(\theta_{0}-\bar{\theta}_{n}^{(1)},0)=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
by assumption. As explained at the start of Section
6.3.4
, it follows from Lemma
30
that (
127
), (
128
) and hence (
129
) still hold for
n^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\hat{\psi}_{n}(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})^{2}=n^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\hat{\psi}_{n,1}(Y_{i}-\tilde{X}_{i}^{\top}\bar{\theta}_{n}^{(1)})
. By analogous reasoning for
j\in\{2,3\}
, we obtain

\hat{\imath}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}\hat{\psi}_{n,j}(Y_{i}-\tilde{X}_{i}^{\top}\bar{\theta}_{n}^{(j)})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{0}^{*}(\varepsilon_{i})^{2}+o_{p}(1)\overset{p}{\to}i^{*}(p_{0}).\qed

Proof of Lemma
16
.

First consider
j=1
. As in the proof of Lemma
15
, the residuals
\check{\varepsilon}_{i}=Y_{i}-X_{i}^{\top}\bar{\beta}_{n}^{(1)}=\varepsilon_{i}+X_{i}^{\top}b_{n}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, where
b_{n}:=\beta_{0}-\bar{\beta}_{n}^{(1)}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
by assumption and hence
\max_{i\in[n]}|X_{i}^{\top}b_{n}|=o_{p}(1)
by (
107
). We have

\biggl|\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\zeta(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}-\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\zeta(\varepsilon_{i})^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggr|\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\bigl(\zeta(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})-\zeta(\varepsilon_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)^{1/2},

and
\zeta
satisfies
(B)
under the hypotheses of Theorem
14
, so similarly to (
127
), it follows from (
136
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\bigl(\zeta(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})-\zeta(\varepsilon_{i})\bigr)^{2}\Bigm|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i\in I_{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\zeta(z+X_{i}^{\top}b_{n})-\zeta(z)\bigr)^{2}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\overset{p}{\to}0.

Together with Lemma
47
and the weak law of large numbers, this implies that
n^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\zeta(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})^{2}=n^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}\zeta(\varepsilon_{i})^{2}+o_{p}(1)\overset{p}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})^{2}\bigr)/3
, so arguing similarly for
j\in\{2,3\}
, we obtain

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})^{2}\overset{p}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(\zeta(\varepsilon_{1})^{2}\bigr)

(130)

as
n\to\infty
. Next, since
\zeta
satisfies
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta(z+t)^{2}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\in\mathbb{R}
for
t\in\{-t_{0},t_{0}\}
, the same is true of
\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}
, so by Lemma
44
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(z+t)\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\in\mathbb{R}
for all
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
and

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(z+t)\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\to\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(z)\,p_{0}(z)\,dz

as
t\to 0
. Moreover, since
-\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
is non-negative and continuous Lebesgue almost everywhere, it follows from Scheffé’s lemma
(e.g.
van der Vaart, 1998
, Lemma 2.29)
that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(z+t)-\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(z)|\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\to 0
as
t\to 0
, so

\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in I_{3}}|\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})-\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(\varepsilon_{i})|\Bigm|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\Bigr)\leq\max_{i\in I_{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(z+X_{i}^{\top}b_{n})-\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(z)|\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\overset{p}{\to}0.

By analogous reasoning for
j\in\{2,3\}
, we deduce that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(\varepsilon_{i})+o_{p}(1)\overset{p}{\to}\mathbb{E}\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(\varepsilon_{1}).

(131)

Finally, for
m\in[M]
and
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, define
\tilde{p}_{n,j}(z_{m}):=|I_{j+2}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{j+2}}K_{h}(z_{m}-\check{\varepsilon}_{i})
and first consider
j=1
. Letting
p_{n,1}(z_{m}):=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\tilde{p}_{n,1}(z_{m})\!\bigm|\!\mathcal{D}_{1}\bigr)
, recall from (
77
) and (
78
) that by the bounded convergence theorem,

\displaystyle|p_{n,1}(z_{m})-p_{0}(z_{m})|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p_{0}(z_{m}-uh-x^{\top}b_{n})-p_{0}(z_{m})|\,K(u)\,du\,dP_{X}(x)\overset{p}{\to}0

\displaystyle\Var\bigl(\tilde{p}_{n,1}(z_{m})\!\bigm|\!\mathcal{D}_{1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|{p_{0}}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K^{2}}{|I_{3}|h}\to 0.

Thus,
\tilde{p}_{n,1}(z_{m})\overset{p}{\to}p_{0}(z_{m})
by Lemma
47
, and similarly
\tilde{p}_{n,j}(z_{m})\overset{p}{\to}p_{0}(z_{m})
for
j\in\{2,3\}
, so
\tilde{p}_{n}(z_{m})=n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{3}|I_{j+2}|\,\tilde{p}_{n,j}(z_{m})\overset{p}{\to}p_{0}(z_{m})
for all
m\in[M]
. Combining this with (
131
) shows that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})-\sum_{m=1}^{M}\zeta_{m}\tilde{p}_{n}(z_{m})\overset{p}{\to}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}\,p_{0}-\sum_{m=1}^{M}\zeta_{m}p_{0}(z_{m})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta,

which together with (
130
) yields

\hat{\upsilon}_{n}:=\frac{n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})^{2}}{\bigl\{n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}(\check{\varepsilon}_{i})-\sum_{m=1}^{M}\zeta_{m}\tilde{p}_{n}(z_{m})\bigr\}^{2}}\overset{p}{\to}\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta^{2}\,p_{0}}{(\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta)^{2}}=\upsilon_{p_{0}}.\qed

6.4
Auxiliary results and proofs

For reference, we provide a formal statement of the classical asymptotic result in (
3
) for generic convex
M
-estimators. Since the error distributions in this paper have uniformly continuous densities
p_{0}
, we only require mild assumptions on the convex loss function
L
.
Our condition
(B)
is simple and neither forces
L
to be differentiable everywhere, nor places any explicit restrictions on the growth of
L(z)
as
|z|\to\infty
.

Proposition 40
.

Consider a random design linear model (
29
) where
\mathbb{E}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\top})
is invertible and the error density
p_{0}
is absolutely continuous with
i(p_{0})<\infty
. If
L\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a convex loss function for which
\varphi=-L^{(\mathrm{R})}
satisfies
(B)
and
\mathbb{E}\varphi(\varepsilon_{1})=0
, then the following statements hold.

(a)

For each
n
, there always exists
\hat{\beta}_{n}\in\argmin_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{\top}\beta)
, and this is unique if the design matrix
X=(X_{1}\;\cdots\;X_{n})^{\top}
has full column rank and
\varphi
is strictly decreasing.

(b)

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\beta}_{n}-\beta_{0})\overset{d}{\to}N_{d}\bigl(0,V_{p_{0}}(\varphi)\cdot\{\mathbb{E}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\top})\}^{-1}\bigr)
, where
V_{p_{0}}(\varphi)=(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{2}\,p_{0})/(\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\varphi)^{2}
is as in (
12
).

A necessary condition for uniqueness in
(a)
is that
X
has full column rank, which happens with probability tending to 1 as
n\to\infty
since
\mathbb{E}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\top})
is invertible. The hypothesis that
p_{0}
is absolutely continuous with
i(p_{0})<\infty
is only used to prove
(b)
, and can be relaxed provided that the function
\Lambda\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
given by (
108
) satisfies (
110
). In particular, this ensures that
\Lambda^{\prime}(0)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\zeta\in(0,\infty)
; see Lemmas
43
and
44
below.

Proof.

(a)
By assumption,
\inf_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\varphi(z)<0<\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\varphi(z)
, so
L
is coercive in the sense that
L(z)\to\infty
as
|z|\to\infty
. Thus,
\theta\equiv(\theta_{1},\dotsc,\theta_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}L(Y_{i}-\theta_{i})=:\mathcal{L}_{n}(\theta)
is convex and coercive on
\mathrm{Im}(X):=\{X\beta:\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
, so
\beta\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{n}(X\beta)
attains its minimum on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
\zeta
is strictly decreasing, then
\mathcal{L}_{n}
is strictly convex on
\mathrm{Im}(X)
, so has a unique minimiser
m\in\mathrm{Im}(X)
. If in addition
X
has full column rank, then there exists a unique
\hat{\beta}_{n}
such that
m=X\hat{\beta}_{n}
, i.e.
\hat{\beta}_{n}=\argmin_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{\top}\beta)
.

(b)
This follows from simpler versions of Lemmas
32
and
33
, whose key steps we now outline; see also
Bickel, 1975
and
He and Shao, 2000
. Define

R_{n}(b):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\Bigl\{\zeta\Bigl(\varepsilon_{i}-\frac{X_{i}^{\top}b}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)-\Lambda\Bigl(\frac{X_{i}^{\top}b}{\sqrt{n}}\Bigr)\Bigr\}

for
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then for every
M>0
, we deduce by almost identical arguments to those in the proof of Lemma
36
that
\sup_{b\in B_{M}}\|{R_{n}(b)-R_{n}(0)}\|=o_{p}(1)
as
n\to\infty
, where
B_{M}=\{b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|{b}\|\leq M\}
. Consequently, imitating the proofs of Lemmas
33
and
37
yields

\bigl\{\|{\hat{\beta}_{n}-\beta_{0}}\|>t\bigr\}\subseteq\biggl\{\inf_{u\in S^{d-1}}u^{\top}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\zeta(\varepsilon_{i}-tX_{i}^{\top}u)\leq 0\biggr\},

which together with (
110
) allows us to conclude that
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\beta}_{n}-\beta_{0})=O_{p}(1)
and
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{n})=o_{p}(1)
. With these ingredients in place, the rest of the proof proceeds very similarly to that of Theorem
13
, except with
\zeta
in place of both
\hat{\psi}_{n,j}^{\mathrm{anti}}
and
\psi_{0}^{*}
, and
\Lambda
instead of
\hat{\Psi}_{n,j}^{\mathrm{anti}}
.
∎

Huber, 1964
established an equivalent variational characterisation of the Fisher information for information; see
Huber and Ronchetti, 2009
for an alternative proof of the following fact based on Hilbert space theory.

Proposition 41
.

For a distribution
P_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

P_{0}
has an absolutely continuous density
p_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}
with respect to Lebesgue measure, with
i(p_{0})=\int_{\{p_{0}>0\}}(p_{0}^{\prime})^{2}/p_{0}<\infty
.

(ii)

I(P_{0}):=\displaystyle\sup_{\psi}\frac{\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi^{\prime}\,dP_{0}\bigr)^{2}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi^{2}\,dP_{0}}<\infty
,
where the supremum is taken over all compactly supported, continuously differentiable
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi^{2}\,dP_{0}>0
.

Furthermore, if either (i) or (ii) holds, then
I(P_{0})=i(p_{0})
.

The proofs of Lemma
30
and the results in Section
6.3.4
require the next four lemmas, the first of which is adapted from
van der Vaart, 1998
.

Lemma 42
.

Suppose that
p_{0}
is an absolutely continuous density on
\mathbb{R}
with
i(p_{0})<\infty
. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, let
p_{t}(\cdot):=p_{0}(\cdot+t)
, so that
p_{t}^{\prime}(\cdot)=p_{0}^{\prime}(\cdot+t)
. Then the location model
\{p_{t}:t\in\mathbb{R}\}
is differentiable in quadratic mean at every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, i.e.

\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{p_{t+h}}-\sqrt{p_{t}}}{h}+\frac{p_{t}^{\prime}}{2\sqrt{p_{t}}}\biggr)^{2}=0

for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\sqrt{p_{t+h}}-\sqrt{p_{t}})^{2}=O(h^{2})
as
h\to 0
.

Proof.

Under our assumptions on
p_{0}
, we will show first that
\sqrt{p_{0}}
is locally absolutely continuous on
\mathbb{R}
with derivative
p_{0}^{\prime}/(2\sqrt{p_{0}})
Lebesgue almost everywhere. This essentially follows from
Serrin and Varberg, 1969
, but we give a direct argument here for completeness. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\varphi_{n},\Phi_{n}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\varphi_{n}(z):=1/(2\sqrt{z})
for
z>1/n
and
\varphi_{n}(z):=0
otherwise, and
\Phi_{n}(z):=\int_{0}^{z}\varphi_{n}
. Then for every
z\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\Phi_{n}(z)\nearrow z_{+}^{1/2}
and
\varphi_{n}(z)\nearrow 1/(2\sqrt{z})\mathbbm{1}_{\{z>0\}}=:\varphi(x)
as
n\to\infty
. Now
p_{0}
is absolutely continuous on
\mathbb{R}
and each
\Phi_{n}
is Lipschitz on
\mathbb{R}
with weak derivative
\varphi_{n}
, so
\Phi_{n}\circ p_{0}
is absolutely continuous on
\mathbb{R}
for each
n
, with
(\Phi_{n}\circ p_{0})^{\prime}(z)=(\varphi_{n}\circ p_{0})(z)\cdot p_{0}^{\prime}(z)
for Lebesgue almost every
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

(\Phi_{n}\circ p_{0})(y)-(\Phi_{n}\circ p_{0})(x)=\int_{x}^{y}(\varphi_{n}\circ p_{0})\,p_{0}^{\prime},

and by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,
\bigl(\int_{x}^{y}|(\varphi\circ p_{0})\,p_{0}^{\prime}|\bigr)^{2}\leq|y-x|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl((\varphi\circ p_{0})\,p_{0}^{\prime}\bigr)^{2}=|y-x|\,i(p_{0})/4<\infty
. It follows by the dominated convergence theorem that

\displaystyle\sqrt{p_{0}(y)}-\sqrt{p_{0}(x)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl((\Phi_{n}\circ p_{0})(y)-(\Phi_{n}\circ p_{0})(x)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{x}^{y}(\varphi_{n}\circ p_{0})\,p_{0}^{\prime}=\int_{x}^{y}(\varphi\circ p_{0})\,p_{0}^{\prime}=\int_{x}^{y}\frac{p_{0}^{\prime}}{2\sqrt{p_{0}}}

(132)

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, so
\sqrt{p_{0}}
is indeed locally absolutely continuous on
\mathbb{R}
.

Thus, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and Lebesgue almost every
z\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{p_{t+h}(z)}-\sqrt{p_{t}(z)}}{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{p_{0}(z+t+h)}-\sqrt{p_{0}(z+t)}}{h}

\displaystyle\to\frac{p_{0}^{\prime}(z+t)}{2\sqrt{p_{0}(z+t)}}=\frac{p_{t}^{\prime}(z)}{2\sqrt{p_{t}(z)}}=:q_{t}(z)

(133)

as
h\to 0
. Moreover, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
h\neq 0
, we deduce from (
132
), the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and Fubini’s theorem that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{p_{t+h}(z)}-\sqrt{p_{t}(z)}}{h}\biggr)^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}q_{t+sh}(z)\,ds\biggr)^{2}dz

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{1}q_{t+sh}(z)^{2}\,ds\,dz=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{p_{t+sh}^{\prime}(z)^{2}}{4p_{t+sh}(z)}\,dz\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{p_{t}^{\prime}(z)^{2}}{4p_{t}(z)}\,dz\,ds=\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{t}^{2}(z)\,dz=\frac{i(p_{0})}{4}<\infty.

(134)

It follows from (
6.4
), (
134
) and
van der Vaart, 1998
that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{p_{t+h}}-\sqrt{p_{t}}}{h}-q_{t}\biggr)^{2}\to 0

as
h\to 0
. Therefore,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{p_{t+h}}-\sqrt{p_{t}}}{h}\biggr)^{2}\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{p_{t+h}}-\sqrt{p_{t}}}{h}-q_{t}\biggr)^{2}+2\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{t}^{2}=O(1)

as
h\to 0
, as required.
∎

Lemma 43
.

Let
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be decreasing and right-continuous, and let
g\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function of bounded variation such that
\lim_{|z|\to\infty}g(z)=0
. Suppose that there exists
t_{0}>0
such that

\varPsi(t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z-t)\,g(z)\,dz\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(z-t)\,d\psi(z)

are finite for
t\in\{-t_{0},t_{0}\}
. Then
the same is true of all
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
, and
t\mapsto\varPsi(t)
is differentiable at 0 with

\varPsi^{\prime}(0)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g\,d\psi=-\lim_{t\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(z-t)\,d\psi(z)\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

Since
\psi
is decreasing,
|\psi(z-t)|\leq|\psi(z-t_{0})|+|\psi(z+t_{0})|=:\psi_{*}(z)
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
, so because
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{*}(z)\,g(z)\,dz<\infty
, it follows that
\varPsi(t)\in\mathbb{R}
for all
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
. Moreover, since
\lim_{|z|\to\infty}g(z)=0
, we have
\int_{(-\infty,t)}dg=-\int_{[t,\infty)}dg=g(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Define
h(z,t):=\mathbbm{1}_{\{z<t\leq 0\}}-\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<t\leq z\}}
for
z,t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(z,t-t_{0})\,d\psi(t)=\psi(t_{0})-\psi(z+t_{0})
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(z,t-t_{0})\,dg(z)=g(t-t_{0})
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Since
\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t-t_{0})\,d\psi(t)\in\mathbb{R}
, it follows from Fubini’s theorem that

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t-t_{0})\,d\psi(t)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(z,t-t_{0})\,dg(z)\,d\psi(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\psi(z+t_{0})-\psi(t_{0})\bigr)\,dg(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z+t_{0})\,dg(z).

(135)

Similarly,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z-t_{0})\,dg(z)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t+t_{0})\,d\psi(t)\in\mathbb{R}
, so
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{*}\,dg\in\mathbb{R}
. We can find increasing, continuous functions
g_{1},g_{2}
such that
g=g_{1}-g_{2}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{*}\,d(g_{1}+g_{2})<\infty

(e.g.
Folland, 1999
, p. 94 and 103–104)
.
Therefore, for all
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
, we have

\biggl|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z+t)\,dg(z)\biggr|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\psi(z+t)|\,d(g_{1}+g_{2})(z)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{*}\,d(g_{1}+g_{2})<\infty,

so it follows similarly to (
135
) that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z+t)\,dg(z)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(z-t)\,d\psi(z)\in\mathbb{R}
. For all
z\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]\setminus\{0\}
, we have
|\psi(z+t)|\vee|t^{-1}\int_{z}^{z+t}\psi|\leq\psi_{*}(z)
. Moreover, denoting by
A
the set of continuity points of
\psi
, we have
\lim_{t\to 0}\psi(z+t)=\psi(z)=\lim_{t\to 0}t^{-1}\int_{z}^{z+t}\psi
for all
z\in A
. Since
\mathbb{R}\setminus A
is countable and
g
is continuous, it follows from Fubini’s theorem and the dominated convergence theorem that for
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
,

-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(z-t)\,d\psi(z)=\int_{A}\psi(z+t)\,dg(z)\to\int_{A}\psi(z)\,dg(z)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g\,d\psi

as
t\to 0
. For
t\in(0,t_{0}]
, a further application of Fubini’s theorem yields

\displaystyle\frac{\varPsi(t)-\varPsi(0)}{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{g(z+t)-g(z)}{t}\,\psi(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{A}\mathbbm{1}_{\{z<s\leq z+t\}}\,dg(s)\,\psi(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\frac{1}{t}\int_{s-t}^{s}\psi(z)\,dz\,dg(s),

which converges to
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi\,dg=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g\,d\psi
as
t\searrow 0
by the dominated convergence theorem. Similarly, again for
t\in(0,t_{0}]
, we have

\frac{\varPsi(0)-\varPsi(-t)}{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{z-t<s\leq z\}}\,dg(s)\,\psi(z)\,dz=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{s}^{s+t}\psi(z)\,dz\,dg(s)\searrow-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g\,d\psi,

as required.
∎

Lemma 44
.

Let
p_{0}
be an absolutely continuous density with
i(p_{0})<\infty
, and define
p_{t}(\cdot):=p_{0}(\cdot+t)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a decreasing, right-continuous function for which there exists
t_{0}>0
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z-t)^{2}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz<\infty
for
t\in\{-t_{0},t_{0}\}
. Then

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z)^{2}\bigl(\sqrt{p_{t}(z)}-\sqrt{p_{0}(z)}\bigr)^{2}\,dz\to 0\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\psi(z-t)-\psi(z)\bigr)^{2}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\to 0

(136)

as
t\to 0
. Moreover,
\varPsi(t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z-t)\,p_{0}(z)\,dz
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}(z-t)\,d\psi(z)

are finite for all
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
, and

\lim_{v\to 0}\,\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in[-v,v]:\\
s\neq t\end{subarray}}\,\biggl|\frac{\varPsi(t)-\varPsi(s)}{t-s}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\psi\biggr|=0,

(137)

where
-\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\psi=-\lim_{t\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}(z-t)\,d\psi(z)=\varPsi^{\prime}(0)\in[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

Since
\psi
is decreasing, it is continuous Lebesgue almost everywhere and
\psi(z-t)^{2}\leq\psi(z-t_{0})^{2}\vee\psi(z+t_{0})^{2}
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
. Thus, by the dominated convergence theorem,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi^{2}\,p_{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z-t)^{2}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\to\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi^{2}\,p_{0}
as
t\to 0
. Hence by the continuity of
p_{0}
and a slight generalisation of Scheffé’s lemma
(
van der Vaart, 1998
, Lemma 2.29)
, we obtain (
136
).

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,
|\varPsi(t)|\leq(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z-t)^{2}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz)^{1/2}<\infty
for all
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
, and

\displaystyle\bigl\{\varPsi(t)-\varPsi(s)-\bigl(\varPsi(t-s)-\varPsi(0)\bigr)\bigr\}^{2}

\displaystyle=\biggl\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\psi(z-s)-\psi(z)\bigr)\bigl(p_{0}(z+t-s)-p_{0}(z)\bigr)\,dz\biggr\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\psi(z-s)-\psi(z)\bigr)^{2}(p_{t-s}+p_{0})(z)\,dz\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\sqrt{p_{t-s}}-\sqrt{p_{0}}\bigr)^{2}

for
s,t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
.
Moreover,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\psi(z-s)-\psi(z)\bigr)^{2}p_{t-s}(z)\,dz=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\psi(z-t)-\psi(z+s-t)\bigr)^{2}p_{0}(z)\,dz
, so by the second limit in (
136
) and the final assertion of Lemma
42
,

\lim_{v\to 0}\,\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in[-v,v]:\\
s\neq t\end{subarray}}\,\biggl|\frac{\varPsi(t)-\varPsi(s)-\bigl(\varPsi(t-s)-\varPsi(0)\bigr)}{t-s}\biggr|=0.

(138)

We have
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p_{0}^{\prime}|\leq i(p_{0})^{1/2}<\infty
, so
p_{0}
is of bounded variation on
\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, since
p_{0}
is a density, we have
\lim_{|z|\to\infty}p_{0}(z)=0
, so writing
\psi_{0}=p_{0}^{\prime}/p_{0}
, it follows from (
135
) and Cauchy–Schwarz that

\displaystyle 0\leq-\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}(z+t)\,d\psi(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z-t)p_{0}^{\prime}(z)\,dz=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z-t)\,\psi_{0}(z)\,p_{0}(z)\,dz

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(z-t)^{2}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\biggr)^{1/2}i(p_{0})^{1/2}<\infty

for
t\in\{-t_{0},t_{0}\}
. Therefore, by Lemma
43
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}(z+t)\,d\psi(z)
is finite for all
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
and
\varPsi^{\prime}(0)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\psi=-\lim_{t\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}(z-t)\,d\psi(z)\in[0,\infty)
, so

\lim_{v\to 0}\,\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in[-v,v]:\\
s\neq t\end{subarray}}\,\biggl|\frac{\varPsi(t-s)-\varPsi(0)}{t-s}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0}\,d\psi\biggr|=0,

which together with (
138
) yields (
137
).
∎

Lemma 45
.

Let
p_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
be a Lebesgue density. For
h>0
, define
p_{0,h}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty]
by
p_{0,h}(z):=(2h)^{-1}\int_{z-h}^{z+h}p_{0}
. Given
\varepsilon_{1},\dotsc,\varepsilon_{n}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}p_{0}
and a kernel
K\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
that is supported on
[-1,1]
, define
\hat{p}_{n,h}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
by
\hat{p}_{n,h}(z):=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K_{h}(z-\varepsilon_{i})
, where
K_{h}(\cdot)=h^{-1}K(\cdot/h)
. Then for
\rho\in[0,1/2]
and
\delta>\rho/(1-\rho)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{p}_{n,h}(z)-\mathbb{E}\hat{p}_{n,h}(z)|\,dz

\displaystyle\leq\|{K}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\min\biggl\{\frac{p_{0,h}^{1/2}}{(nh)^{1/2}},2p_{0,h}\biggr\}\leq\frac{2^{1-2\rho}\|{K}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0,h}^{1-\rho}}{(nh)^{\rho}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{1-2\rho}\|{K}\|_{\infty}C_{\delta,\rho}^{\rho}(1+h)^{\delta(1-\rho)}\bigl(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}}|z|^{\delta}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\bigr)^{1-\rho}}{(nh)^{\rho}},

where
C_{\rho,\delta}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|z|)^{-\delta(1-\rho)/\rho}\,dz=2\rho/\bigl((1-\rho)\delta-\rho\bigr)\in(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

For
z\in\mathbb{R}
and
h>0
, we have

\mathbb{E}|\hat{p}_{n,h}(z)|=\mathbb{E}|K_{h}(z-\varepsilon_{1})|=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|K(u)|\,p_{0}(z-uh)\,du\leq\|{K}\|_{\infty}\,p_{0,h}(z)

and similarly

\Var\hat{p}_{n,h}(z)=\frac{\Var K_{h}(z-\varepsilon_{1})}{n}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\{K_{h}(z-\varepsilon_{1})^{2}\}}{n}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{K(u)^{2}}{nh}\,p_{0}(z-uh)\,du\leq\frac{\|{K}\|_{\infty}^{2}\,p_{0,h}(z)}{nh},

so

\mathbb{E}|\hat{p}_{n,h}(z)-\mathbb{E}\hat{p}_{n,h}(z)|\leq\min\bigl\{\Var^{1/2}\hat{p}_{n,h}(z),2\mathbb{E}|\hat{p}_{n,h}(z)|\bigr\}\leq\|{K}\|_{\infty}\min\biggl\{\frac{p_{0,h}(z)^{1/2}}{(nh)^{1/2}},2p_{0,h}(z)\biggr\}.

By Fubini’s theorem and the fact that
\min(a,b)\leq a^{2\rho}b^{1-2\rho}
for
a,b\geq 0
and
\rho\in[0,1/2]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{p}_{n,h}(z)-\mathbb{E}\hat{p}_{n,h}(z)|\,dz

\displaystyle\leq\|{K}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\min\biggl\{\frac{p_{0,h}(z)^{1/2}}{(nh)^{1/2}},2p_{0,h}(z)\biggr\}\,dz

\displaystyle\leq\|{K}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\biggl(\frac{p_{0,h}(z)}{nh}\biggr)^{\rho}\bigl(2p_{0,h}(z)\bigr)^{1-2\rho}\,dz=\frac{2^{1-2\rho}\|{K}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0,h}^{1-\rho}}{(nh)^{\rho}}.

Finally, if
U\sim U[-1,1]
is independent of
\varepsilon_{1}
, then
hU+\varepsilon_{1}
has density
p_{0,h}
, so by Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{0,h}^{1-\rho}

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|z|)^{-\delta(1-\rho)/\rho}\,dz\biggr)^{\rho}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|z|)^{\delta}\,p_{0,h}(z)\,dz\biggr)^{1-\rho}

\displaystyle=C_{p,\delta}^{\rho}\,\bigl[\mathbb{E}\bigl\{(1+|hU+\varepsilon_{1}|)^{\delta}\bigr\}\bigr]^{1-\rho}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\delta}^{\rho}\,\bigl[\mathbb{E}\bigl\{(1+h|U|)^{\delta}\cdot(1+|\varepsilon_{1}|)^{\delta}\bigr\}\bigr]^{1-\rho}\leq C_{p,\delta}^{\rho}(1+h)^{\delta(1-\rho)}\biggl(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}}|z|^{\delta}\,p_{0}(z)\,dz\biggr)^{1-\rho}.

This completes the proof.
∎

As an application of Lemma
45
, when
p_{0}
is a Cauchy density and
h>8/n
, taking
\rho=1/2-1/\log(nh)
and
\delta=2\rho=1-2/\log(nh)
in Lemma
45
yields a bound on
\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{p}_{n,h}(z)-\mathbb{E}\hat{p}_{n,h}(z)|\,dz
of order
\log(nh)/\sqrt{nh}
, which is tight up to a universal constant.

Lemma 46
.

Let
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a linear subspace of dimension
d<n
. If
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is countable, then

\mathcal{A}:=\Bigl\{(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{y_{i}-w_{i}\in A\}}\geq d+1\text{ for some }(w_{1},\dotsc,w_{n})\in W\Bigr\}

has Lebesgue measure 0.

Proof.

For
I\subseteq[n]
with
|I|=d+1
, denote by
E_{I}
the linear span of
(\mathsf{e}_{i}:i\in I^{c})
, which has dimension
n-d-1
. Then
E_{I}+W=\{z+w:z\in E_{I},\,w\in W\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a linear subspace of dimension at most
(n-d-1)+d=n-1
, so it is a null set (i.e. has Lebesgue measure 0). Moreover,
A_{I}:=\{\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\mathsf{e}_{i}:a_{i}\in A\text{ for all }i\in I\}
is countable. Therefore,

\mathcal{A}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[n]\\
|I|=d+1\end{subarray}}\bigcup_{a\in A_{I}}(a+E_{I}+W)

is a countable union of null sets, and hence is also a null set.
∎

Lemma 47

(
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
, Lemma 6.1)
.

Suppose that
(X_{n})
is a sequence of random vectors and
(\mathcal{G}_{n})
is a sequence of
\sigma
-algebras. If
\mathbb{E}(\|{X_{n}}\|\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{n})=o_{p}(1)
, then
\|{X_{n}}\|=o_{p}(1)
as
n\to\infty
. Similarly, if
\mathbb{E}(\|{X_{n}}\|\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{n})=O_{p}(1)
, then
\|{X_{n}}\|=O_{p}(1)
.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. If
\mathbb{E}(\|{X_{n}}\|\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{n})=o_{p}(1)
, then
\mathbb{P}(\|{X_{n}}\|>\varepsilon\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{n})\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\mathbb{E}(\|{X_{n}}\|\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{n})\overset{p}{\to}0
by Markov’s inequality, so the bounded convergence theorem implies that
\mathbb{P}(\|{X_{n}}\|>\varepsilon)=\mathbb{E}\,\mathbb{P}(\|{X_{n}}\|>\varepsilon\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, we conclude that
\|{X_{n}}\|=o_{p}(1)
.

On the other hand, suppose that
\mathbb{E}(\|{X_{n}}\|\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{n})=O_{p}(1)
. Then for any sequence
M_{n}\to\infty
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\|{X_{n}}\|>M_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{n})\leq M_{n}^{-1}\mathbb{E}(\|{X_{n}}\|\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{n})=o_{p}(1)
by Markov’s inequality, so similarly
\mathbb{P}(\|{X_{n}}\|>M_{n})=\mathbb{E}\,\mathbb{P}(\|{X_{n}}\|>M_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Thus,
\|{X_{n}}\|=O_{p}(1)
.
∎

Lemma 48
.

Let
(\Delta_{n})
be a sequence of random measurable functions
8
8

8

More precisely, writing
\Omega
for the underlying probability space, suppose that there exist jointly measurable functions
\tilde{\Delta}_{n}\colon\mathbb{R}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\Delta_{n}(v)(\cdot)=\tilde{\Delta}_{n}(v,\cdot)\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}
.
from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}
. Assume that for some deterministic sequence
(w_{n})
with
w_{n}\to 0
, we have
\Delta_{n}(v_{n})\overset{p}{\to}0
as
n\to\infty
whenever
(v_{n})
is a deterministic sequence such that
v_{n}=o(w_{n})
. Then for any sequence of random variables
(V_{n})
independent of
(\Delta_{n})
, we have
\Delta_{n}(V_{n})\overset{p}{\to}0
whenever
V_{n}=o_{p}(w_{n})
.

Proof.

Fix
\epsilon>0
and let
g_{n}(v):=\mathbb{P}(|\Delta_{n}(v)|>\epsilon)
for
v\in\mathbb{R}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By assumption,
g_{n}(v_{n})\to 0
whenever
v_{n}=o(w_{n})
. Since
(V_{n})
and
(\Delta_{n})
are independent, we have
\mathbb{P}\bigl(|\Delta_{n}(V_{n})|>\epsilon\,|\,V_{n}\bigr)=g_{n}(V_{n})\overset{p}{\to}0
whenever
V_{n}=o_{p}(w_{n})
.
Therefore, by the bounded convergence theorem,
\mathbb{P}(|\Delta_{n}(V_{n})|>\epsilon)=\mathbb{E}\,\mathbb{P}\bigl(|\Delta_{n}(V_{n})|>\epsilon\,|\,V_{n}\bigr)\to 0
, as required.
∎

6.5
Antitonic projections and least concave majorants

Denote by
\Psi_{\downarrow}
the set of all decreasing functions on
\mathbb{R}
. For an integrable function
f\colon(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
, recall from Section
1.2
the definitions of
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{L}}f
and
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{R}}f
on
[0,1]
.

Proposition 49

(
Samworth and Shah, 2025
, Exercise 9.24
)
.

If
\psi\in L^{2}(P)
for some Borel probability measure
P
on
\mathbb{R}
with distribution function
F
, then

\psi_{P}^{*}:=\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{L}}(\psi\circ F^{-1})\circ F\in\argmin_{g\in\Psi_{\downarrow}}\,\int_{I}(g-\psi)^{2}\,dP=:\Pi_{\downarrow}(\psi,P).

(139)

We have
\psi_{P}^{*}\in L^{2}(P)
, and moreover
\psi^{\star}\in\Pi_{\downarrow}(\psi,P)
if and only if
\psi^{\star}=\psi_{P}^{*}

P
-almost everywhere. Furthermore, if
F
is continuous, then also
\psi_{P}^{**}:=\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{R}}(\psi\circ F^{-1})\circ F\in\Pi_{\downarrow}(\psi,P)
.

We refer to
\Pi_{\downarrow}(\psi,P)
as the
L^{2}(P)
antitonic
(decreasing isotonic)
projection
of
\psi
. Since
\Psi_{\downarrow}
is a convex class of functions,

\int_{I}(g-\psi_{P}^{*})(\psi-\psi_{P}^{*})\,dP\leq 0\;\;\text{for all }g\in\Psi_{\downarrow}\text{ and }\psi_{P}^{*}\in\Pi_{\downarrow}(\psi,P);

(140)

see e.g.
Samworth and Shah, 2025
. This can be used to derive the basic inequality (
141
) below. For a measurable function
g
on
\mathbb{R}
, let
\|{g}\|_{L^{\infty}(P)}:=\sup\bigl\{\lambda\geq 0:P(\{z\in\mathbb{R}:|g(z)|\geq\lambda\})>0\bigr\}
.

Lemma 50
.

Given Borel probability measures
P,Q
on
\mathbb{R}
and
\psi\in L^{2}(P)\cap L^{2}(Q)
, let
\psi_{P}^{*}\in\Pi_{\downarrow}(\psi,P)
and
\psi_{Q}^{*}\in\Pi_{\downarrow}(\psi,Q)
. Then

\|{\psi_{P}^{*}-\psi_{Q}^{*}}\|_{L^{2}(P)}^{2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\psi-\psi_{Q}^{*})(\psi_{Q}^{*}-\psi_{P}^{*})\,d(Q-P).

(141)

Moreover, if
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in L^{2}(P)
and
\psi_{\ell}^{\star}\in\Pi_{\downarrow}(\psi_{\ell},P)
for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, then

\displaystyle\|{\psi_{1}^{\star}-\psi_{2}^{\star}}\|_{L^{2}(P)}

\displaystyle\leq\|{\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}}\|_{L^{2}(P)},

(142)

and writing
\psi_{\ell}^{*}\equiv(\psi_{\ell})_{P}^{*}
and
\psi_{\ell}^{**}\equiv(\psi_{\ell})_{P}^{**}
for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we have

\displaystyle\|{\psi_{1}^{*}-\psi_{2}^{*}}\|_{\infty}=\|{\psi_{1}^{**}-\psi_{2}^{**}}\|_{\infty}\leq\|{\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}(P)}.

(143)

Proof.

By (
140
),

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\psi_{Q}^{*}-\psi_{P}^{*})(\psi-\psi_{P}^{*})\,dP\leq 0\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\psi_{P}^{*}-\psi_{Q}^{*})(\psi_{Q}^{*}-\psi)\,dQ,

and adding
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,(\psi_{Q}^{*}-\psi_{P}^{*})(\psi_{Q}^{*}-\psi)\,dP
to both sides yields (
141
).

For the second assertion, define

\displaystyle D(t)

\displaystyle:=\bigl\|(1-t)\psi_{1}^{\star}+t\psi_{1}-\{(1-t)\psi_{2}^{\star}+t\psi_{2}\}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(P)}^{2}=\bigl\|(\psi_{1}^{\star}-\psi_{2}^{\star})+t(\psi_{1}-\psi_{1}^{\star}+\psi_{2}^{\star}-\psi_{2})\bigr\|_{L^{2}(P)}^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{:}=\|{\psi_{1}^{\star}-\psi_{2}^{\star}}\|_{L^{2}(P)}^{2}+2t\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\psi_{1}^{\star}-\psi_{2}^{\star})(\psi_{1}-\psi_{1}^{\star}+\psi_{2}^{\star}-\psi_{2})\,dP+t^{2}\|{\psi_{1}-\psi_{1}^{\star}+\psi_{2}^{\star}-\psi_{2}}\|_{L^{2}(P)}^{2},

which is a quadratic function of
t\in\mathbb{R}
. By (
140
),
\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\psi_{1}^{\star}-\psi_{2}^{\star})(\psi_{1}-\psi_{1}^{\star}+\psi_{2}^{\star}-\psi_{2})\,dP\geq 0
, so
D
is non-decreasing on
[0,\infty)
. Thus,
\|{\psi_{1}^{\star}-\psi_{2}^{\star}}\|_{L^{2}(P)}^{2}=D(0)\leq D(1)=\|{\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}}\|_{L^{2}(P)}^{2}
, which proves (
142
).

For
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in L^{2}(P)
, it follows from the min-max formulae for the isotonic projection
(
Samworth and Shah, 2025
, Exercise 9.25
(b)
and Theorem 9.37)
that,

\varphi_{1}\leq\varphi_{2}\;\;P\text{-almost everywhere}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\varphi_{1}^{*}\leq\varphi_{2}^{*}\;\;P\text{-almost everywhere}.

(144)

Now defining
d:=\|{\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}(P)}
, we have
\psi_{1}-d\leq\psi_{2}\leq\psi_{1}+d

P
-almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}
, so (
144
) implies that
\psi_{1}^{*}-d=(\psi_{1}-d)^{*}\leq\psi_{2}^{*}\leq(\psi_{1}+d)^{*}=\psi_{1}^{*}+d

P
-almost everywhere. In other words,
|\psi_{1}^{*}-\psi_{2}^{*}|\leq d
on a set of the form
F^{-1}(E):=\{F^{-1}(u):u\in E\}
for some
E\subseteq(0,1)
with Lebesgue measure 1.

It remains to show that
|\psi_{1}^{*}-\psi_{2}^{*}|\vee|\psi_{1}^{**}-\psi_{2}^{**}|\leq d
on
\mathbb{R}
. For
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, define
\Psi_{\ell}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
by
\Psi_{\ell}(u):=\int_{0}^{u}\psi_{\ell}\circ F^{-1}
. Fix
z\in\mathbb{R}
and suppose first that
u:=F(z)\in(0,1]
. Since
E
is dense in
[0,1]
, we can find a sequence
(u_{n})
in
E
such that
u_{n}\nearrow u
and hence
u_{n}\leq(F\circ F^{-1})(u_{n})\nearrow(F\circ F^{-1})(u)=u
. Therefore, since
\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{(\mathrm{L})}-\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{(\mathrm{L})}
is left-continuous on
(0,1]
by
Rockafellar, 1997
, we have

|\psi_{1}^{*}(z)-\psi_{2}^{*}(z)|=\bigl|\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{(\mathrm{L})}(u)-\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{(\mathrm{L})}(u)\bigr|=\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl|\psi_{1}^{*}\bigl(F^{-1}(u_{n})\bigr)-\psi_{2}^{*}\bigl(F^{-1}(u_{n})\bigr)\bigr|\leq d.

Next, when
u\in[0,1)
, we can find a sequence
(u_{n})
in
E
such that
u_{n}\searrow u
. Let
u^{\prime}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}F\bigl(F^{-1}(u_{n})\bigr)
.
If
u^{\prime}=u
, then by
Rockafellar, 1997
,

\displaystyle|\psi_{1}^{**}(z)-\psi_{2}^{**}(z)|=\bigl|\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{(\mathrm{R})}(u)-\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{(\mathrm{R})}(u)\bigr|

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl|\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{(\mathrm{L})}\bigl((F\circ F^{-1})(u_{n})\bigr)-\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{(\mathrm{L})}\bigl((F\circ F^{-1})(u_{n})\bigr)\bigr|

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl|\psi_{1}^{*}\bigl(F^{-1}(u_{n})\bigr)-\psi_{2}^{*}\bigl(F^{-1}(u_{n})\bigr)\bigr|\leq d.

(145)

Otherwise, if
u^{\prime}>u
, then
(F\circ F^{-1})(u^{\prime})=u^{\prime}
and
F^{-1}(v)=F^{-1}(u^{\prime})
for all
v\in(u,u^{\prime}]
. Thus, for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, it follows that
\psi_{\ell}\circ F^{-1}
is constant and hence
\Psi_{\ell},\hat{\Psi}_{\ell}
are both affine on
[u,u^{\prime}]
with
\hat{\Psi}_{\ell}^{(\mathrm{R})}(u)=\hat{\Psi}_{\ell}^{(\mathrm{L})}(u^{\prime})
, so (
145
) remains valid. Since
|\psi_{1}^{*}(z)-\psi_{2}^{*}(z)|=|\psi_{1}^{**}(z)-\psi_{2}^{**}(z)|
when
u=F(z)\in\{0,1\}
, the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 51
.

For a function
F\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, suppose that its least concave majorant
\hat{F}
on
[0,1]
satisfies
F(v)<\hat{F}(v)<\infty
for some
v\in(0,1)
at which
F
is continuous. Then there exists
\delta\in\bigl(0,\min(v,1-v)\bigr)
such that
\hat{F}
is affine on
[v-\delta,v+\delta]
.

Proof.

Since
